{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}\mathsf{1}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}^{\prime}
for some
\vec{Y}^{\prime}
s.t.
\vec{Y}^{\prime}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\varepsilon
. Since
\mathit{word}(\mathsf{Skip})=\varepsilon
, we have
(\mathsf{Skip},\vec{Y}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

(
Case

T=\mathtt{\llparenthesis}\ell{:}\,T\mathtt{\rrparenthesis}_{\ell\in L}
): By rules
L-RcdVrt
and
L-RcdVrtField
, the LTS at
T
has exactly the transitions
T\overset{\llparenthesis\rrparenthesis_{\checkmark}}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Skip}
and
T\overset{\llparenthesis\rrparenthesis_{k}}{\longrightarrow}T_{k}
for each
k\in L
. Similarly, the LTS at
\vec{X}_{T}
has exactly the transitions
\vec{X}_{T}\overset{\llparenthesis\rrparenthesis_{\checkmark}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\bot
and
\vec{X}_{T}\overset{\llparenthesis\rrparenthesis_{k}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(T_{k})
for each
k\in L
. Since
\vec{X}_{T}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, the LTS at
\vec{Y}
has exactly the transitions
\vec{Y}\overset{\llparenthesis\rrparenthesis_{k}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{k}
for some
\vec{Y}_{k}
s.t.
\mathit{word}(T_{k})\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
for each
k\in L
and additionally
\vec{Y}\overset{\llparenthesis\rrparenthesis_{\checkmark}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{\checkmark}
for some
\vec{Y}_{\checkmark}
s.t.
\vec{Y}_{\checkmark}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\bot
. It follows that
(T_{k},\vec{Y}_{k})\in\mathcal{R}
for each
k\in L
. Observe that, since neither
\varepsilon
or
\bot
have any transitions, we have
\varepsilon\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\bot
. From this and
\mathit{word}(\mathsf{Skip})=\varepsilon
it follows that
(\mathsf{Skip},\vec{Y}_{\checkmark})\in\mathcal{R}
.

(
Case

T=x
): Cannot occur, since
\not\vdash x
.

(
Case

T=\mathsf{End}
): by rule
L-End
, the LTS at
T
has the unique transition
T\overset{\mathsf{End}}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Skip}
. Similarly, the LTS at
\vec{X}_{T}
has the unique transition
\vec{X}_{T}\overset{\mathsf{End}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\bot
. Since
\vec{X}_{T}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, the LTS at
\vec{Y}
has the unique transition
\vec{Y}\overset{\mathsf{End}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}^{\prime}
for some
\vec{Y}
such that
\vec{Y}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\bot
. Since
\mathit{word}(\mathsf{Skip})=\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\bot
, we have
\mathit{word}(\mathsf{Skip})\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}^{\prime}
and therefore
(\mathsf{Skip},\vec{Y}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

(
Case

T={\sharp}U
): By rule
L-Msg
, the LTS at
T
has exactly two transitions
T\overset{\sharp\mathsf{p}}{\longrightarrow}U
and
T\overset{\sharp\mathsf{c}}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Skip}
. Similarly, the LTS at
\vec{X}_{T}
has exactly two transitions
\vec{X}_{T}\overset{\sharp\mathsf{p}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(U)\bot
and
\vec{X}_{T}\overset{\sharp\mathsf{c}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\varepsilon
. Since
\vec{X}_{T}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, the LTS at
\vec{Y}
has exactly two transitions
\vec{Y}\overset{\sharp\mathsf{p}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{1}
and
\vec{Y}\overset{\sharp\mathsf{c}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{2}
for some
\vec{Y}_{1},\vec{Y}_{2}
s.t.
\mathit{word}(U)\bot\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{1}
and
\varepsilon\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{2}
. By Lemma
27
, we have
\mathit{word}(U)\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(U)\bot\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{1}
, and by the definition of
\mathit{grm}
,
\mathit{word}(\mathsf{Skip})\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\varepsilon\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{2}
. Hence
(U,\vec{Y}_{1}),(\mathsf{Skip},\vec{Y}_{2})\in\mathcal{R}
.

(
Case

T={\odot}\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,T\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}
): By rules
L-RcdVrt
and
L-RcdVrtField
, the LTS at
T
has exactly the transitions
T\overset{\odot_{\checkmark}}{\longrightarrow}\mathsf{Skip}
and
T\overset{\odot_{k}}{\longrightarrow}T_{k}
for each
k\in L
. Similarly, the LTS at
\vec{X}_{T}
has transitions
\vec{X}_{T}\overset{\odot_{\checkmark}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\bot
and
\vec{X}_{T}\overset{\odot_{k}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(T_{k})
for each
k\in L
. Since
\vec{X}_{T}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, the LTS at
\vec{Y}
has exactly the transitions
\vec{Y}\overset{\odot_{\checkmark}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{\checkmark}
for some
\vec{Y}_{\checkmark}
s.t.
\vec{Y}_{\checkmark}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\bot
and
\vec{Y}\overset{\odot_{k}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{k}
for some
\vec{Y}_{k}
s.t.
\mathit{word}(T_{k})\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{k}
for each
k\in L
. Since
\bot
has no productions, we have
\bot\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\varepsilon
and by transitivity
\vec{Y}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\varepsilon
. Hence
(\mathsf{Skip},\vec{Y}_{\checkmark})\in\mathcal{R}
and
(T_{k},\vec{Y}_{k})\in\mathcal{R}
for each
k\in L
.

(
Case

T={\sharp U}{;}{S}
): By rules
L-MsgSeq1
and
L-MsgSeq2
, the LTS at
T
has exactly two transitions,
T\overset{\sharp\mathsf{p}}{\longrightarrow}U
and
T\overset{\sharp\mathsf{c}}{\longrightarrow}S
. Given that
\mathit{word}(T)=\mathit{word}(\sharp U)\cdot\mathit{word}(S)
and that
\mathit{word}(\sharp U)
yields exactly two productions
\mathit{word}(\sharp U)\to\sharp\mathsf{p}\mathit{word}(U)\bot
and
\mathit{word}(\sharp U)\to\sharp\mathsf{c}\mathit{word}(S)
, we have for the LTS at
\vec{X}_{T}
exactly two transitions
X_{T}\overset{\sharp\mathsf{p}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(U)\bot\mathit{word}(S)
and
X_{T}\overset{\sharp\mathsf{c}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(S)
. Since
X_{T}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, the LTS at
\vec{Y}
has exactly two transitions
\vec{Y}\overset{\sharp\mathsf{p}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{1}
and
\vec{Y}\overset{\sharp\mathsf{c}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{2}
for some
\vec{Y}_{1},\vec{Y}_{2}
s.t.
\mathit{word}(U)\bot\mathit{word}(S)\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{1}
and
\mathit{word}(S)\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{2}
. By Lemma
27
, we have
\mathit{word}(U)\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(U)\bot\mathit{word}(S)\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}_{1}
. Hence
(U,\vec{Y}_{1}),(S,\vec{Y}_{2})\in\mathcal{R}
.

(
Case

T={x}{;}{S}
): Cannot occur, since
\not\vdash{x}{;}{S}
.

Now we consider the cases in which
T\neq\mathit{unr}(T)
. It is straightforward to check that the LTS at
T
has a transition
T\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}U
iff the LTS at
\mathit{unr}(T)
has a corresponding transition
\mathit{unr}(T)\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}U
(with the same
U
). This is a consequence of the fact that the LTS rules for
T
essentially follow its unfolding, which eventually terminates due to contractivity. On the side of grammars, we have
\mathit{word}(\mathit{unr}(T))\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(T)\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
. Now suppose that
T\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}U
; then
\mathit{unr}(T)\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}U
; since
\mathit{word}(\mathit{unr}(T))\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, our previous case analysis yields
\vec{Y}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}\vec{Y}^{\prime}
for some
\vec{Y}^{\prime}
s.t.
\mathit{word}(U)\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}^{\prime}
; concluding that
(U,\vec{Y}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
. Conversely, suppose that
\vec{Y}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}^{\prime}
for some
\vec{Y}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathit{word}(\mathit{unr}(T))\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, our previous case analysis yields
\mathit{unr}(T)\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}U
for some
U
s.t.
\mathit{word}(U)\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}^{\prime}
. We conclude that
T\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}U
and therefore that
(U,\vec{Y}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.
∎

We are now able to prove
\cref
thm:soundness-grammars.
See
14

Proof.

Consider the following relation on pairs of types

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\{(V,W)\,|\,\vec{X}_{V}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W}\}\text{,}

where
\vdash V
,
\vdash W
,
(\vec{X}_{V},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})=\mathit{grm}(V,\emptyset)
and
(\vec{X}_{W},\mathcal{P})=\mathit{grm}(W,\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
.

To prove the desired property, we show that
\mathcal{R}\subseteq{\lesssim}
, in other words, that
\mathcal{R}
is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation with
\mathcal{X,Y,Z,W}
defined as the sets generated, respectively, by the grammars for
a_{\mathcal{X}},a_{\mathcal{Y}},a_{\mathcal{Z}},a_{\mathcal{W}}
in Definition
8
. Take
(V,W)\in\mathcal{R}
with
(\vec{X}_{V},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})=\mathit{grm}(V,\emptyset)
and
(\vec{X}_{W},\mathcal{P})=\mathit{grm}(W,\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
.

First, let there be
a\in\mathcal{X}
and
V^{\prime}
s.t.
V\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}V^{\prime}
. We want to show that there is a
W^{\prime}
s.t.
W\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}W^{\prime}
and
(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
. By Lemma
28
, there exists
\vec{Y}
such that
\vec{X}_{V}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
and
\mathit{word}(V^{\prime})\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
. Since
\vec{X}_{V}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W}
, we know there exists a matching word
\vec{Z}
such that
\vec{X}_{W}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
and that
\vec{Y}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
. Again by Lemma
28
, there exists
W^{\prime}
such that
W\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}W^{\prime}
and
\vec{Z}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(W^{\prime})
. Let
(\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})=\mathit{grm}(V^{\prime},\emptyset)
and
(\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}},\mathcal{P})=\mathit{grm}(W^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
, and recall that
\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}=\mathit{word}(V^{\prime})
and
\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}=\mathit{word}(W^{\prime})
. Having
\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
and
\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
, we know by Lemma
26
that
\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
and
\vec{Z}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}
. Recalling that
\vec{Y}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
, we establish by transitivity that
\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}
and therefore that
(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

Next, let there be
a\in\mathcal{Y}
and
W^{\prime}
s.t.
W\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}W^{\prime}
. We want to show that there is a
V^{\prime}
s.t.
V\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}V^{\prime}
and
(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
. Lemma
28
gives us some
\vec{Z}
with
\vec{X}_{W}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
and
\mathit{word}(W^{\prime})\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
. Since
a\in\mathcal{Y}
, we know from
\vec{X}_{V}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W}
that there is a matching word
\vec{Y}
such that
\vec{X}_{V}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
and that
\vec{Y}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
. Once again by Lemma
28
we know there exists
V^{\prime}
such that
V\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}V^{\prime}
and
\vec{Y}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(V^{\prime})
. Let
(\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})=\mathit{grm}(V^{\prime},\emptyset)
and
(\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}},\mathcal{P})=\mathit{grm}(W^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
. Having
\vec{Y}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}
and
\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
, we know by Lemma
26
that
\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
and
\vec{Z}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}
. Recalling that
\vec{Y}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
, we can establish by transitivity that
\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}
and therefore that
(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

Now let there be
a\in\mathcal{Z}
and
V^{\prime}
s.t.
V\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}V^{\prime}
. We want to show that there is a
W^{\prime}
s.t.
W\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}W^{\prime}
and
(W^{\prime},V^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
. By Lemma
28
, there exists
\vec{Y}
s.t.
\vec{X}_{V}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}\vec{Y}
and
\mathit{word}(V^{\prime})\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
. Since
\vec{X}_{V}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W}
, we know there exists a matching word
\vec{Z}
s.t.
\vec{X}_{W}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}\vec{Z}
and that
\vec{Z}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
. Again by Lemma
28
, there exists
W^{\prime}
s.t.
W\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}W^{\prime}
and
\vec{Z}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(W^{\prime})
. Let
(\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})=\mathit{grm}(V^{\prime},\emptyset)
and
(\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}},\mathcal{P})=\mathit{grm}(W^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
, and recall that
\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}=\mathit{word}(V^{\prime})
and
\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}=\mathit{word}(W^{\prime})
. Having
\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
and
\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, we know by Lemma
26
that
\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
and
\vec{Y}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}
. Recalling that
\vec{Z}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, we establish by transitivity that
\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}
and therefore that
(W^{\prime},V^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.

Finally, let there be
a\in\mathcal{W}
and
W^{\prime}
s.t.
W\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}W^{\prime}
. We want to show that there is a
V^{\prime}
s.t.
V\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}V^{\prime}
and
(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
. Lemma
28
gives us some
\vec{Z}
with
\vec{X}_{W}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
and
\mathit{word}(W^{\prime})\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
. Since
a\in\mathcal{Y}
, we know from
\vec{X}_{V}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W}
that there is a matching word
\vec{Y}
such that
\vec{X}_{V}\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
and that
\vec{Z}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
. Once again by Lemma
28
we know there exists
V^{\prime}
such that
V\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}V^{\prime}
and
\vec{Y}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\mathit{word}(V^{\prime})
. Let
(\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})=\mathit{grm}(V^{\prime},\emptyset)
and
(\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}},\mathcal{P})=\mathit{grm}(W^{\prime},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
. Having
\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
and
\vec{Y}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}
, we know by Lemma
26
that
\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
and
\vec{Y}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}
. Recalling that
\vec{Z}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
, we can establish by transitivity that
\vec{X}_{V^{\prime}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{X}_{W^{\prime}}
and therefore that
(V^{\prime},W^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}
.
∎

Appendix E
Pruning

The grammars generated by procedure
\mathit{grm}
may contain unreachable words, which can be ignored by the algorithm. Intuitively, these words correspond to communication actions that cannot be fulfilled, such as part
{{?}\mathsf{Bool}}
in type
{(\mu{s}{.}{{{!}\mathsf{Int}}}{;}{s})}{;}{{{?}\mathsf{Bool}}}
. Formally, these words appear in productions following what are known as
unnormed words
.

Definition 29
.

Let
\vec{a}
be a non-empty sequence of non-terminal symbols
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
. Write
\vec{Y}\overset{\vec{a}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
when
\vec{Y}\overset{a_{1}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\ldots\overset{a_{n}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
. We say that a word
\vec{Y}
is normed if
\vec{Y}\overset{\vec{a}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\varepsilon
for some
\vec{a}
, and unnormed otherwise. If
\vec{Y}
is normed and
\vec{a}
is the shortest path such that
\vec{Y}\overset{\vec{a}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\varepsilon
, then
\vec{a}
is called the minimal path of
\vec{Y}
, and its length is the norm of
\vec{Y}
, denoted
\mathit{norm}(\vec{Y})
.

It is known that any unnormed word
\vec{Y}
is bisimilar to its concatenation with any other word, i.e., if
\vec{Y}
is unnormed, then
\vec{Y}\sim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}\vec{X}
. It is easy to show that
{\sim_{\mathcal{P}}}\subseteq{\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}}
, and hence that
\vec{Y}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}\vec{X}
. In this case,
\vec{X}
is said to be unreachable and can be safely removed from the grammar. We call the procedure of removing all unreachable symbols from a grammar
pruning
, and denote the pruned version of a grammar
\mathcal{P}
by
\mathit{prune}(\mathcal{P})
.
See
16

Proof.

For the direct implication, the
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation for
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}
over
\mathcal{P}
is also an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation for
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}
over
\mathit{prune}(\mathcal{P})
. For the reverse implication, if
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation for
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}
over
\mathit{prune}(P)
, then relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\{(\vec{V}W,\vec{V}W\vec{Z})\,|\,(W\to\vec{V}W\vec{Z})\in\mathcal{P},W\,\text{unnormed}\}

is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation for
\vec{X}
and
\vec{Y}
over
\mathcal{P}
.
∎

Appendix F
Proof of Theorem
20

To prove the soundness of our algorithm, we need to prove the soundness of its three phases: translation from types to grammars, grammar pruning and exploration of an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-expansion tree (with
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{W}
instantiated as in
\cref
def:hocfst-sim). The soundness of the first two phases is given by
\cref
thm:soundness-grammars and
\cref
thm:pruning-preserves-xyzw, respectively. It remains now to prove that the last phase is also sound.

The exploration of the
\mathcal{XYZW}
-expansion tree is carried out through expansion and simplification steps. Expansion steps attempt to build an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation from an initial pair of grammar words
(\vec{X},\vec{Y})
, while simplification steps are used modify the construction of the tree, attempting to keep some branches of the tree finite even when the corresponding
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation is not. This procedure returns
True
whenever an empty node is reached (meaning there is a successful branch), or
False
if all nodes fail to expand (meaning there is no successful branch). Given these stopping conditions, the soundness of the exploration procedure relies on the tree it yields having the following property: there is a successful branch iff
\vec{X}\preceq^{\mathcal{X}\mathcal{Y}\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{W}}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Y}
. This is known as the
safeness property
.

The proof of this property is given in two parts: in the first we show the property holds for a tree constructed through expansion steps only; in the second we show that the simplification rules modify the tree safely, i.e., if their application results in a tree with a successful branch, then an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation can actually be constructed.
See
18

Proof.

In an expansion tree without simplification both directions follow directly from the definition of expansion. Recall that in an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-expansion tree, each child is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-expansion of its parent. Observe then that in an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-expansion tree rooted at
\{(\vec{X},\vec{Y})\}
, the union of all nodes along a successful branch (i.e. infinite or containing an empty leaf) constitutes an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation (over productions
\mathcal{P}
) that includes
(\vec{X},\vec{Y})
. Hence
\vec{X}\lesssim_{\mathcal{P}}Y
.

In an expansion tree with simplifications, the union of all nodes along a successful branch need not be an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation, only a (hopefully finite) representation of it, i.e., a set with which we can reconstruct it by reversing the simplifications. It remains to show how this can be done for each simplification rule:

•

Reflexivity
: Let
N
be a node at depth
n
such that
\{(\vec{X}_{i},\vec{X}_{i})\}_{i\in 1..j}\subseteq N
for some
j
. Applying
Reflexivity
, its simplification is
N^{\prime}=N\setminus\{(\vec{X}_{i},\vec{X}_{i})\}_{i\in 1..j}
. Observe that the reflexive closure of the union of all nodes along the successful branch containing
N^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation containing
N
.

•

Preorder
: Let
\leq_{N}
be the least preorder containing the ancestors of a node
N
. Applying
Preorder
, its simplification is
N^{\prime}=N\setminus{\leq_{N}}
. Observe that the reflexive and transitive closure of the union of all nodes along the successful branch containing
N^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation containing
N
.

•

Split
: Let
N
be a node containing a pair of the form
(X_{0}\vec{X},Y_{0}\vec{Y})
with
\mathit{norm}(X_{0})\leq\mathit{norm}(Y_{0})
(the case where
\mathit{norm}(X_{0})>\mathit{norm}(Y_{0})
is similar). Let sequence
\vec{a}=a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
be a minimal path for
X_{0}
, and
\vec{Z}
be a word such that
Y_{0}\overset{\vec{a}}{\longrightarrow}_{\mathcal{P}}\vec{Z}
. Applying
Split
to
N
yields
N
itself and a sibling
N^{\prime}
with pairs
(X_{0}\vec{Z},Y_{0}),(\vec{X},\vec{Z}\vec{Y})
in place of
(X_{0}\vec{X},Y_{0}\vec{Y})
. We need to show that, assuming there is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation over
\mathcal{P}
containing
N^{\prime}
, it is possible to obtain an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation over
\mathcal{P}
containing
N
.

Let
\mathcal{R^{\prime}}
be an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation over
\mathcal{P}
containing
N^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}_{1}
be the smallest
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation over
\mathcal{P}
that includes pair
(X_{0}\vec{Z},Y_{0})
. Then, relation

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R^{\prime}}\cup\{(X_{0}\vec{X},Y_{0}\vec{Y})\}\cup\{(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{X},\vec{Y}_{1}\vec{Y})\mathrel{|}(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{Z},\vec{Y}_{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\}

is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation over
\mathcal{P}
containing
N
.

Since
N^{\prime}
includes all pairs of
N
except
(X_{0}\vec{X},Y_{0}\vec{Y})
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation over
\mathcal{P}
containing
N^{\prime}
, it follows from the union of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
with
\{(X_{0}\vec{X},Y_{0}\vec{Y})\}
that
\mathcal{R}
contains
N
.

All that remains now is to show that
\mathcal{R}
is an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation over
\mathcal{P}
. Since
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is already such a relation, we need to show that it remains so after adding to it every pair in
\{(X_{0}\vec{X},Y_{0}\vec{Y})\}\cup\{(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{X},\vec{Y}_{1}\vec{Y})\mathrel{|}(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{Z},\vec{Y}_{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\}
. For
(X_{0}\vec{X},Y_{0}\vec{Y})
this is easy: we observe that the pairs containing the derivatives of all matching transitions of
X_{0}\vec{X}
and
Y_{0}\vec{Y}
are elements of
\{(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{X},\vec{Y}_{1}\vec{Y})\mathrel{|}(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{Z},\vec{Y}_{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\}
. For the pairs in
\{(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{X},\vec{Y}_{1}\vec{Y})\mathrel{|}(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{Z},\vec{Y}_{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\}
we need to distinguish two cases:

–

(
Case

\vec{X}_{1}\neq\varepsilon
): We observe that, from the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, it follows that relation
\{(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{X},\vec{Y}_{1}\vec{Y})\mathrel{|}(\vec{X}_{1}\vec{Z},\vec{Y}_{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\}
, which is contained in
\mathcal{R}
, contains the paired derivatives of all matching transitions of
X_{1}\vec{X}
and
Y_{1}\vec{Y}
.

–

(
Case

\vec{X}_{1}=\varepsilon
): We observe that, since
\mathcal{R}
contains
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
(an
\mathcal{XYZW}
-simulation containing
N^{\prime}
), it must include the paired derivatives of all matching transitions of
\vec{X}
and
Y_{1}\vec{Y}
.

∎

With the safeness property established, we can finally put together a soundness proof encompassing all of the three phases of the algorithm.

Lemma 30
.

If
\mathit{subG}(\vec{X}_{T},\vec{X}_{U},\mathit{prune}(\mathcal{P}))
returns
True
, then
\vec{X}_{T}\lesssim_{\mathit{prune}(\mathcal{P})}\vec{X}_{U}
.

Proof.

Function
\mathit{subG}
returns
True
whenever it finds a finite successful branch (i.e., a branch terminating in
an empty node) in the expansion tree rooted at
\{(\vec{X},\vec{Y})\}
. Conclude with the safeness property,
\cref
lemma:safeness.
∎

See
20

Proof.

From
\cref
thm:soundness-grammars,
\cref
thm:pruning-preserves-xyzw and
\cref
thm:soundness-pruned.
∎

Appendix G
Generating subtyping pairs

We rely on a number of properties of subtyping to generate valid and invalid subtyping pairs. Before enumerating these properties, we introduce the following definition.

Definition 31
.

The sets of free references in covariant and contravariant positions in a type
T
, respectively
\mathsf{freeCov}(T)
and
\mathsf{freeContrav}(T)
, are mutually defined by induction on the structure of
T
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeCov}(T\overset{m}{\rightarrow}U)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeContrav}(T)\cup\mathsf{freeCov}(U)

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeCov}(\mathtt{\llparenthesis}\ell{:}\,T_{\ell}\mathtt{\rrparenthesis}_{\ell\in L})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{k\in L}\mathsf{freeCov}(T_{k})

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeCov}({{?}T})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeCov}(T)

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeCov}({{!}T})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeContrav}(T)

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeCov}({S}{;}{R})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeCov}(S)\cup\mathsf{freeCov}(R)

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeCov}(\mu{x}{.}T)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeCov}(T)\setminus\{x\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeCov}(x)

\displaystyle=\{x\}

(and in all other cases by
\mathsf{freeCov}(T)=\emptyset
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeContrav}(T\overset{m}{\rightarrow}U)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeCov}(T)\cup\mathsf{freeContrav}(U)

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeContrav}(\mathtt{\llparenthesis}\ell{:}\,T_{\ell}\mathtt{\rrparenthesis}_{\ell\in L})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{k\in L}\mathsf{freeContrav}(T_{k})

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeContrav}({{?}T})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeContrav}(T)

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeContrav}({{!}T})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeCov}(T)

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeContrav}({S}{;}{R})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeContrav}(S)\cup\mathsf{freeContrav}(R)

\displaystyle\mathsf{freeContrav}(\mu{x}{.}T)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{freeContrav}(T)\setminus\{x\}

(and in all other cases by
\mathsf{freeContrav}(T)=\emptyset
)

The following theorem enumerates the properties of the subtyping relation. From it we can derive generation algorithm for valid subtyping pairs, parameterized on the size
i
of the pair:
if
i=0
, then select one of the pairs in
\cref
thm:props-base; if
i\geq 1
, then select one of the pairs in the remaining items.

Theorem 32

(Properties of subtyping (valid))
.

1.

\mathsf{Unit}\leq\mathsf{Unit}
,
\mathsf{End}\leq\mathsf{End}
and
\mathsf{Skip}\leq\mathsf{Skip}
;

2.

T\overset{m}{\rightarrow}U\leq V\overset{n}{\rightarrow}W
if
V\leq T
,
U\leq W
and
m\sqsubseteq n
;

3.

\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,T_{\ell}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}\leq\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,U_{k}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in L}
if
K\subseteq L
and
T_{j}\leq U_{j}(\forall j\in K)
;

4.

\mathtt{\langle}\ell{:}\,T_{\ell}\mathtt{\rangle}_{\ell\in L}\leq\mathtt{\langle}k{:}\,U_{k}\mathtt{\rangle}_{k\in K}
if
L\subseteq K
and
T_{j}\leq U_{j}(\forall j\in L)
;

5.

{S_{1}}{;}{S_{2}}\leq{R_{1}}{;}{R_{2}}
if
S_{1}\leq R_{1}
and
S_{2}\leq R_{2}
;

6.

\mu{x}{.}T\leq\mu{x}{.}U
if:

(a)

T\leq U
and
x\notin\mathit{freeContrav}(T)\cup\mathit{freeContrav}(U)
;

(b)

T\leq U
and
U\leq T
;

7.

\oplus\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,S_{\ell}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}\leq\oplus\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,R_{k}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in L}
if
K\subseteq L
and
S_{j}\leq R_{j}(\forall j\in L)
;

8.

\&\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,S_{\ell}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}\leq\&\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,R_{k}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in K}
if
L\subseteq K
and
S_{j}\leq R_{j}(\forall j\in L)
;

9.

{\mathsf{End}}{;}{S}\leq\mathsf{End}
,
\mathsf{End}\leq{\mathsf{End}}{;}{S}
and
{\mathsf{End}}{;}{S}\leq{\mathsf{End}}{;}{R}
for any
S,R
;

10.

{S}{;}{\mathsf{Skip}}\leq R
,
{\mathsf{Skip}}{;}{S}\leq R
,
S\leq{R}{;}{\mathsf{Skip}}
and
S\leq{\mathsf{Skip}}{;}{R}
if
S\leq R
;

11.

{\oplus\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,S_{\ell}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}}{;}{S^{\prime}}\leq\oplus\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,{R_{k}}{;}{R^{\prime}}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in L}
and
\oplus\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,{S_{\ell}}{;}{S^{\prime}}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}\leq{\oplus\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,R_{k}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in L}}{;}{R^{\prime}}
if
K\subseteq L
,
S_{j}\leq R_{j}(\forall j\in L)
and
S^{\prime}\leq R^{\prime}
;

12.

{\&\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,S_{\ell}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}}{;}{S^{\prime}}\leq\&\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,{R_{k}}{;}{R^{\prime}}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in L}
and
\&\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,{S_{\ell}}{;}{S^{\prime}}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}\leq{\&\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,R_{k}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in L}}{;}{R^{\prime}}
if
L\subseteq K
,
S_{j}\leq R_{j}(\forall j\in L)
and
S^{\prime}\leq R^{\prime}
;

13.

{S_{1}}{;}{({S_{2}}{;}{S_{3}})}\leq{({R_{1}}{;}{R_{2}})}{;}{R_{3}}
and
{({S_{1}}{;}{S_{2}})}{;}{S_{3}}\leq{R_{1}}{;}{({R_{2}}{;}{R_{3}})}
if
S_{1}\leq R_{1}
,
S_{2}\leq R_{2}
and
S_{3}\leq R_{3}
;

14.

\mu{x}{.}T\leq U
if
T\leq U
and
x\notin\mathit{free}(T)
;

15.

T\leq\mu{x}{.}U
if
T\leq U
and
x\notin\mathit{free}(U)
;

16.

\mu{x}{.}T\leq[\mu{y}{.}U/y]U
if
\mu{x}{.}T\leq\mu{y}{.}U
.

Proof.

By observation of the syntactic subtyping rules and the definition of
\mathsf{freeContra}
for
\cref
thm:props-rec.
∎

Identifying the set of references in contravariant positions makes it easier to generate valid subtyping pairs featuring recursion. Observe that, despite looking so,
\mu{t}{.}t\overset{*}{\rightarrow}t
is not a subtype of
\mu{t}{.}t\overset{\mathsf{1}}{\rightarrow}t
(their unfolding makes it clear). This is because the same self-reference appears in both covariant and contravariant positions (hence the first must be simultaneously subtype and supertype of the latter, which cannot happen because of their multiplicities). We avoid generating such pairs in
\cref
thm:props-rec by ensuring that references introduced by a pair of recursive types only appear in covariant positions of their bodies, unless they are equivalent, in which case there is no such restriction.

To generate invalid subtyping pairs, we follow the same algorithm but inject also the invalid pairs that occur in each item of the following theorem.

Theorem 33

(Properties of subtyping (invalid))
.

1.

\mathsf{Int}\not\leq\mathsf{Unit}
,
\mathsf{Unit}\not\leq\mathsf{Int}
,
\mathsf{Skip}\not\leq\mathsf{End}
and

2.

\mathsf{End}\not\leq\mathsf{Skip}
;

3.

\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,T_{\ell}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}\not\leq\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,U_{k}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in L}
if
L\subsetneq K
and
T_{j}\leq U_{j}(\forall j\in L)
;

4.

\mathtt{\langle}\ell{:}\,T_{\ell}\mathtt{\rangle}_{\ell\in L}\not\leq\mathtt{\langle}k{:}\,U_{k}\mathtt{\rangle}_{k\in K}
if
K\subsetneq L
and
T_{j}\leq U_{j}(\forall j\in K)
;

5.

T\overset{\mathsf{1}}{\rightarrow}U\not\leq V\overset{\mathbf{\ast}}{\rightarrow}W
, with
V\leq T,U\leq W
;

6.

T\overset{m}{\rightarrow}U\not\leq V\overset{n}{\rightarrow}W
, with
T\leq V
,
T\not\sim V
,
m\sqsubseteq n
and
U\leq W
;

7.

T\overset{m}{\rightarrow}U\not\leq V\overset{n}{\rightarrow}W
, with
V\leq T
,
m\sqsubseteq n
,
W\leq U
and
W\not\sim U
;

8.

{{?}T}\not\leq{{!}T}
;

9.

{{!}T}\not\leq{{?}T}
;

10.

{{?}T}\not\leq{{?}U}
, with
T\not\sim U,U\leq T
;

11.

{{!}T}\not\leq{{!}U}
, with
T\not\sim U,T\leq U
;

12.

\oplus\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,T_{\ell}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}\not\leq\oplus\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,T_{k}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in K}
if
L\subsetneq K
and
T_{j}\leq U_{j}(\forall j\in L)
;

13.

\&\mathtt{\{}\ell{:}\,T_{\ell}\mathtt{\}}_{\ell\in L}\not\leq\&\mathtt{\{}k{:}\,T_{k}\mathtt{\}}_{k\in K}
if
K\subsetneq L
and
T_{j}\leq U_{j}(\forall j\in K)
.

Proof.

By inspection of the syntactic subtyping rules, relying on the observation that if
T\leq U
and
T\not\sim U
, then
U\not\leq T
.
∎

If
i=1
, we generate one of the pairs in
\cref
item:invalid-1 of
\cref
thm:props-subtyping-invalid, otherwise we use one of the items in
\cref
thm:props-subtyping-valid and randomly inject a subtyping pair where a valid one is supposed to be. If the generated pair turns out to be in the subtyping relation, we simply discard the result.

For example, suppose
i=4
. We randomly choose
\cref
item:valid-5 of
\cref
thm:props-subtyping-valid to generate a pair of sequential compositions
({S_{1}}{;}{R_{1}},{S_{2}}{;}{R_{2}})
. We proceed in the valid path for the types before the semicolon, obtaining
S_{1}={{{!}\mathsf{Int}}}{;}{\mathsf{Skip}}
and
S_{2}={{!}\mathsf{Int}}
, but inject an invalid pair in
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
. To generate it, we randomly choose
\cref
item:arrow1 of
\cref
thm:props-subtyping-valid. Here we generate a valid pair
(T,U)
using
\cref
thm:props-subtyping-valid and ensuring
T
and
V
are not equivalent, then multiplicities
m
and
n
such that
m\sqsubseteq n
, and finally another valid pair
(V,W)
. Thus we obtain
R_{1}=T\overset{m}{\rightarrow}U
and
R_{2}=V\overset{n}{\rightarrow}W
, making
({S_{1}}{;}{R_{1}},{S_{2}}{;}{R_{2}})
an invalid pair. If we had, however, generated
S_{1}=\mathsf{End}
and
S_{2}={\mathsf{End}}{;}{\mathsf{Skip}}
, then
({S_{1}}{;}{R_{1}},{S_{2}}{;}{R_{2}})
would be a valid pair, and its test result would be discarded.
